People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. Well… I guess what brings me in is I’ve been feeling kind of restless. Not all the time, or at least I try not to make a big deal of it, but when I’m supposed to sit still—like in staff meetings or church event briefings—it’s like I’ve got a little motor humming under my skin. I’ll start shifting around, or I need to stand up, or I find some reason to move. I’m not trying to be disruptive or anything… it’s just hard to stay put.

I mean… I can stop if I really notice it, usually. Like if my wife gives me a look when we’re fishing, I’ll put the tackle box down, or at bingo I’ll stop tapping the pencil for a bit.

It’s not just work, I guess. Work is where it shows the most because I’m supposed to be more… organized, you know?

I don’t think it’s usually worry, no… I mean, sometimes I might be a little keyed up like anybody, but most of the time it’s more like my body just won’t settle when I’m supposed to sit still.

I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s not always because I’m scared or apprehensive. It’s more just… sitting still feels hard. Does that make sense?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I’ve been feeling kind of restless. Not all the time, or at least I try not to make a big deal of it, but when I’m supposed to sit still—like in staff meetings or church event briefings—it’s like I’ve got a little motor humming under my skin. I’ll start shifting around, or I need to stand up, or I find some reason to move. I’m not trying to be disruptive or anything… it’s just hard to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you're feeling restless and need to move?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say anxious exactly… I mean, maybe a little uncomfortable, but it’s more like I’ve got this motor humming under my skin when I’m supposed to sit still. Like in staff meetings or church briefings, I start shifting around, or I feel like I need to stand up or find some reason to move. I try not to make it a problem for anybody, but it’s hard to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, kind of. Not like a ritual or anything, I don’t think. But even when I’m supposed to be relaxing—like fishing with my wife—I’ll keep messing with my tackle box, moving things around, tapping a bit. Or at bingo I might tap the pencil or start talking louder than I mean to. \n\nIt’s not terrible, I don’t think, but I do catch myself doing little things over and over when I’m trying to just sit quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about these repetitive actions when you're not doing them?\n\nPatient: Hmm… thinking about them when I’m not doing them? I don’t think so, not really. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these restless feelings and repetitive actions causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, but… yeah, it’s started to cause some trouble, mostly at work. Not huge trouble, I don’t think, but enough that people are noticing.\n\nSometimes during room turnovers or when we’re deciding supplies, I’ll miss part of what someone said, or I’ll jump in too quick, and then it rubs people the wrong way. Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was some terrible kid or anything, but I do remember getting in trouble in grade school for popping out of my seat.

Like in staff meetings or church briefings, I start shifting around, or I feel like I need to stand up or find some reason to move. I try not to make it a problem for anybody, but it’s hard to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, kind of. Not like a ritual or anything, I don’t think. But even when I’m supposed to be relaxing—like fishing with my wife—I’ll keep messing with my tackle box, moving things around, tapping a bit. I’ll start shifting around, or I need to stand up, or I find some reason to move. I’m not trying to be disruptive or anything… it’s just hard to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you're feeling restless and need to move?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say anxious exactly… I mean, maybe a little uncomfortable, but it’s more like I’ve got this motor humming under my skin when I’m supposed to sit still. Like in staff meetings or church briefings, I start shifting around, or I feel like I need to stand up or find some reason to move. I try not to make it a problem for anybody, but it’s hard to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, kind of. Not like a ritual or anything, I don’t think. But even when I’m supposed to be relaxing—like fishing with my wife—I’ll keep messing with my tackle box, moving things around, tapping a bit. I don’t think so, not really. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t remember everything from being that little, but in grade school I did get in trouble for popping up out of my seat and talking over the teacher.

I’d also kind of rush ahead before they were done giving directions. Not trying to be bad, I don’t think… just impatient, I guess. My mother used to say I was “wound too tight” even as a kid. Does that answer what you mean?

Like in staff meetings or church briefings, I start shifting around, or I feel like I need to stand up or find some reason to move. Not like a ritual or anything, I don’t think. But even when I’m supposed to be relaxing—like fishing with my wife—I’ll keep messing with my tackle box, moving things around, tapping a bit. I don’t think so, not really. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, no. It’s not like I have a rule in my head that I have to tap a certain number of times or move a certain way.

It’s more just… I get uncomfortable sitting still, and then I shift or get up before I really think about it. I’m not trying to make it feel “just right,” at least not that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I’ve been feeling kind of restless. Not all the time, or at least I try not to make a big deal of it, but when I’m supposed to sit still—like in staff meetings or church event briefings—it’s like I’ve got a little motor humming under my skin. I’ll start shifting around, or I need to stand up, or I find some reason to move. I’m not trying to be disruptive or anything… it’s just hard to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you're feeling restless and need to move?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say anxious exactly… I mean, maybe a little uncomfortable, but it’s more like I’ve got this motor humming under my skin when I’m supposed to sit still. Like in staff meetings or church briefings, I start shifting around, or I feel like I need to stand up or find some reason to move. I try not to make it a problem for anybody, but it’s hard to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, kind of. Not like a ritual or anything, I don’t think. But even when I’m supposed to be relaxing—like fishing with my wife—I’ll keep messing with my tackle box, moving things around, tapping a bit. Or at bingo I might tap the pencil or start talking louder than I mean to. \n\nIt’s not terrible, I don’t think, but I do catch myself doing little things over and over when I’m trying to just sit quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about these repetitive actions when you're not doing them?\n\nPatient: Hmm… thinking about them when I’m not doing them? I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I might notice afterward like, “Oh, I was tapping again,” but I don’t sit around planning it or worrying about it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more something I catch myself doing in the moment. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these restless feelings and repetitive actions causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, but… yeah, it’s started to cause some trouble, mostly at work. Not huge trouble, I don’t think, but enough that people are noticing.\n\nSometimes during room turnovers or when we’re deciding supplies, I’ll miss part of what someone said, or I’ll jump in too quick, and then it rubs people the wrong way. My supervisor’s had to correct me a few times, and he suggested I talk to the employee assistance program. So… I guess that means it’s affecting things more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over these repetitive actions when you're doing them?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can stop if I really notice it, usually. Like if my wife gives me a look when we’re fishing, I’ll put the tackle box down, or at bingo I’ll stop tapping the pencil for a bit.\n\nBut then, if I’m trying to just sit quiet and relax, I catch myself doing it again—tapping, talking louder than I meant to, fiddling with things. So I guess I have some control, but it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it on purpose. It kind of sneaks back in. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these restless feelings and repetitive actions in most areas of your life, or are they mainly limited to certain situations like work or church?\n\nPatient: It’s not just work, I guess. Work is where it shows the most because I’m supposed to be more… organized, you know?\n\nOn my shifts I’ll go from mopping, to checking trash, to straightening chairs without really stopping, and I tell myself I’m just being productive. But even at home, after work, instead of sitting down, I’ll start puttering in the garage or re-sorting tools.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s kind of in a few places. I don’t mean I’m causing chaos everywhere, but I don’t seem to settle very well. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make any repetitive sounds, like clearing your throat or grunt, when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I clear my throat like anybody would, but I haven’t noticed myself making noises like grunting or anything repetitive like that.\n\nIt’s more something I’d probably have to ask my wife if she’s noticed, but as far as I know, no. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and need to move are usually triggered by feeling worried or apprehensive about something?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s usually worry, no… I mean, sometimes I might be a little keyed up like anybody, but most of the time it’s more like my body just won’t settle when I’m supposed to sit still.\n\nLike in staff meetings, or those church event briefings, I’ll be trying to pay attention and be respectful, but it feels like there’s this motor humming under my skin. I’ll shift around, or stand up if I can, or find some excuse to go move something or check on something.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s not always because I’m scared or apprehensive. It’s more just… sitting still feels hard. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these restless feelings and need to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was some terrible kid or anything, but I do remember getting in trouble in grade school for popping out of my seat.\n\nTeachers would tell me to stop talking over them, or I’d start doing something before they’d even finished giving the directions. My mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” like I just had too much motor in me even then.\n\nSo, yes… I guess it was there when I was a kid too. Is that the kind of childhood stuff you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive actions in response to some kind of thought or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: Not really like a thought, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s not like I’m doing it because I have some idea I can’t get out of my head.\n\nIt’s more just that feeling like I’ve got a motor going under my skin when I’m supposed to sit still. In meetings or those church briefings, I’ll start shifting around, or I’ll stand if I can, or I’ll find some little reason to move. I guess it feels like an urge in my body more than a thought, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're doing these repetitive actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean… I don’t feel like if I don’t shift around or tap my foot then something bad is going to happen.\n\nIt’s more just uncomfortable to sit still, if that makes sense. I’m not trying to stop some disaster or anything like that. I just get restless and end up moving before I really think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and repetitive actions are attempts to reduce anxiety or anxiety-like feelings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s to reduce anxiety, exactly. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I’ve been feeling kind of restless. Not all the time, or at least I try not to make a big deal of it, but when I’m supposed to sit still—like in staff meetings or church event briefings—it’s like I’ve got a little motor humming under my skin. I’ll start shifting around, or I need to stand up, or I find some reason to move. I’m not trying to be disruptive or anything… it’s just hard to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you're feeling restless and need to move?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say anxious exactly… I mean, maybe a little uncomfortable, but it’s more like I’ve got this motor humming under my skin when I’m supposed to sit still. Like in staff meetings or church briefings, I start shifting around, or I feel like I need to stand up or find some reason to move. I try not to make it a problem for anybody, but it’s hard to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, kind of. Not like a ritual or anything, I don’t think. But even when I’m supposed to be relaxing—like fishing with my wife—I’ll keep messing with my tackle box, moving things around, tapping a bit. Or at bingo I might tap the pencil or start talking louder than I mean to. \n\nIt’s not terrible, I don’t think, but I do catch myself doing little things over and over when I’m trying to just sit quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about these repetitive actions when you're not doing them?\n\nPatient: Hmm… thinking about them when I’m not doing them? I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I might notice afterward like, “Oh, I was tapping again,” but I don’t sit around planning it or worrying about it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more something I catch myself doing in the moment. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these restless feelings and repetitive actions causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, but… yeah, it’s started to cause some trouble, mostly at work. Not huge trouble, I don’t think, but enough that people are noticing.\n\nSometimes during room turnovers or when we’re deciding supplies, I’ll miss part of what someone said, or I’ll jump in too quick, and then it rubs people the wrong way. My supervisor’s had to correct me a few times, and he suggested I talk to the employee assistance program. So… I guess that means it’s affecting things more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over these repetitive actions when you're doing them?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can stop if I really notice it, usually. Like if my wife gives me a look when we’re fishing, I’ll put the tackle box down, or at bingo I’ll stop tapping the pencil for a bit.\n\nBut then, if I’m trying to just sit quiet and relax, I catch myself doing it again—tapping, talking louder than I meant to, fiddling with things. So I guess I have some control, but it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it on purpose. It kind of sneaks back in. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these restless feelings and repetitive actions in most areas of your life, or are they mainly limited to certain situations like work or church?\n\nPatient: It’s not just work, I guess. Work is where it shows the most because I’m supposed to be more… organized, you know?\n\nOn my shifts I’ll go from mopping, to checking trash, to straightening chairs without really stopping, and I tell myself I’m just being productive. But even at home, after work, instead of sitting down, I’ll start puttering in the garage or re-sorting tools.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s kind of in a few places. I don’t mean I’m causing chaos everywhere, but I don’t seem to settle very well. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make any repetitive sounds, like clearing your throat or grunt, when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I clear my throat like anybody would, but I haven’t noticed myself making noises like grunting or anything repetitive like that.\n\nIt’s more something I’d probably have to ask my wife if she’s noticed, but as far as I know, no. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and need to move are usually triggered by feeling worried or apprehensive about something?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s usually worry, no… I mean, sometimes I might be a little keyed up like anybody, but most of the time it’s more like my body just won’t settle when I’m supposed to sit still.\n\nLike in staff meetings, or those church event briefings, I’ll be trying to pay attention and be respectful, but it feels like there’s this motor humming under my skin. I’ll shift around, or stand up if I can, or find some excuse to go move something or check on something.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s not always because I’m scared or apprehensive. It’s more just… sitting still feels hard. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these restless feelings and need to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was some terrible kid or anything, but I do remember getting in trouble in grade school for popping out of my seat.\n\nTeachers would tell me to stop talking over them, or I’d start doing something before they’d even finished giving the directions. My mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” like I just had too much motor in me even then.\n\nSo, yes… I guess it was there when I was a kid too. Is that the kind of childhood stuff you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive actions in response to some kind of thought or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: Not really like a thought, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s not like I’m doing it because I have some idea I can’t get out of my head.\n\nIt’s more just that feeling like I’ve got a motor going under my skin when I’m supposed to sit still. In meetings or those church briefings, I’ll start shifting around, or I’ll stand if I can, or I’ll find some little reason to move. I guess it feels like an urge in my body more than a thought, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're doing these repetitive actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I’ve been feeling kind of restless. Not all the time, or at least I try not to make a big deal of it, but when I’m supposed to sit still—like in staff meetings or church event briefings—it’s like I’ve got a little motor humming under my skin. I’ll start shifting around, or I need to stand up, or I find some reason to move. I’m not trying to be disruptive or anything… it’s just hard to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you're feeling restless and need to move?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say anxious exactly… I mean, maybe a little uncomfortable, but it’s more like I’ve got this motor humming under my skin when I’m supposed to sit still. Like in staff meetings or church briefings, I start shifting around, or I feel like I need to stand up or find some reason to move. I try not to make it a problem for anybody, but it’s hard to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, kind of. Not like a ritual or anything, I don’t think. But even when I’m supposed to be relaxing—like fishing with my wife—I’ll keep messing with my tackle box, moving things around, tapping a bit. Or at bingo I might tap the pencil or start talking louder than I mean to. \n\nIt’s not terrible, I don’t think, but I do catch myself doing little things over and over when I’m trying to just sit quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about these repetitive actions when you're not doing them?\n\nPatient: Hmm… thinking about them when I’m not doing them? I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I might notice afterward like, “Oh, I was tapping again,” but I don’t sit around planning it or worrying about it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more something I catch myself doing in the moment. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these restless feelings and repetitive actions causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, but… yeah, it’s started to cause some trouble, mostly at work. Not huge trouble, I don’t think, but enough that people are noticing.\n\nSometimes during room turnovers or when we’re deciding supplies, I’ll miss part of what someone said, or I’ll jump in too quick, and then it rubs people the wrong way. My supervisor’s had to correct me a few times, and he suggested I talk to the employee assistance program. So… I guess that means it’s affecting things more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over these repetitive actions when you're doing them?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can stop if I really notice it, usually. Like if my wife gives me a look when we’re fishing, I’ll put the tackle box down, or at bingo I’ll stop tapping the pencil for a bit.\n\nBut then, if I’m trying to just sit quiet and relax, I catch myself doing it again—tapping, talking louder than I meant to, fiddling with things. So I guess I have some control, but it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it on purpose. It kind of sneaks back in. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these restless feelings and repetitive actions in most areas of your life, or are they mainly limited to certain situations like work or church?\n\nPatient: It’s not just work, I guess. Work is where it shows the most because I’m supposed to be more… organized, you know?\n\nOn my shifts I’ll go from mopping, to checking trash, to straightening chairs without really stopping, and I tell myself I’m just being productive. But even at home, after work, instead of sitting down, I’ll start puttering in the garage or re-sorting tools.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s kind of in a few places. I don’t mean I’m causing chaos everywhere, but I don’t seem to settle very well. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make any repetitive sounds, like clearing your throat or grunt, when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I clear my throat like anybody would, but I haven’t noticed myself making noises like grunting or anything repetitive like that.\n\nIt’s more something I’d probably have to ask my wife if she’s noticed, but as far as I know, no. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and need to move are usually triggered by feeling worried or apprehensive about something?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s usually worry, no… I mean, sometimes I might be a little keyed up like anybody, but most of the time it’s more like my body just won’t settle when I’m supposed to sit still.\n\nLike in staff meetings, or those church event briefings, I’ll be trying to pay attention and be respectful, but it feels like there’s this motor humming under my skin. I’ll shift around, or stand up if I can, or find some excuse to go move something or check on something.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s not always because I’m scared or apprehensive. It’s more just… sitting still feels hard. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these restless feelings and need to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was some terrible kid or anything, but I do remember getting in trouble in grade school for popping out of my seat.\n\nTeachers would tell me to stop talking over them, or I’d start doing something before they’d even finished giving the directions. My mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” like I just had too much motor in me even then.\n\nSo, yes… I guess it was there when I was a kid too. Is that the kind of childhood stuff you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive actions in response to some kind of thought or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: Not really like a thought, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s not like I’m doing it because I have some idea I can’t get out of my head.\n\nIt’s more just that feeling like I’ve got a motor going under my skin when I’m supposed to sit still. In meetings or those church briefings, I’ll start shifting around, or I’ll stand if I can, or I’ll find some little reason to move. I guess it feels like an urge in my body more than a thought, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're doing these repetitive actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean… I don’t feel like if I don’t shift around or tap my foot then something bad is going to happen.\n\nIt’s more just uncomfortable to sit still, if that makes sense. I’m not trying to stop some disaster or anything like that. I just get restless and end up moving before I really think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and repetitive actions are attempts to reduce anxiety or anxiety-like feelings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s to reduce anxiety, exactly. I mean, I can get uncomfortable if I’m stuck sitting still too long, but it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to calm myself down from worry, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… my body just wants to move. I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it. I guess there can be some tension in it, but I haven’t really noticed it being tied to anxious thoughts or anything like that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit these restless behaviors before you were 7 years old?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t remember everything from being that little, but in grade school I did get in trouble for popping up out of my seat and talking over the teacher.\n\nI’d also kind of rush ahead before they were done giving directions. Is that what you’re getting at?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers notice you exhibiting these restless behaviors or repetitive actions before you started elementary school?\n\nPatient: Before elementary school, I’m not totally sure what teachers would’ve seen, if any.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.8, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… I guess what brings me in is I’ve been feeling kind of restless. Not all the time, or at least I try not to make a big deal of it, but when I’m supposed to sit still—like in staff meetings or church event briefings—it’s like I’ve got a little motor humming under my skin. I’ll start shifting around, or I need to stand up, or I find some reason to move. I’m not trying to be disruptive or anything… it’s just hard to stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or stressed when you're feeling restless and need to move?\n\nPatient: I wouldn’t say anxious exactly… I mean, maybe a little uncomfortable, but it’s more like I’ve got this motor humming under my skin when I’m supposed to sit still. Like in staff meetings or church briefings, I start shifting around, or I feel like I need to stand up or find some reason to move. I try not to make it a problem for anybody, but it’s hard to just stay put.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions over and over when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, kind of. Not like a ritual or anything, I don’t think. But even when I’m supposed to be relaxing—like fishing with my wife—I’ll keep messing with my tackle box, moving things around, tapping a bit. Or at bingo I might tap the pencil or start talking louder than I mean to. \n\nIt’s not terrible, I don’t think, but I do catch myself doing little things over and over when I’m trying to just sit quiet.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself thinking about these repetitive actions when you're not doing them?\n\nPatient: Hmm… thinking about them when I’m not doing them? I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I might notice afterward like, “Oh, I was tapping again,” but I don’t sit around planning it or worrying about it, if that’s what you mean.\n\nIt’s more something I catch myself doing in the moment. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Are these restless feelings and repetitive actions causing problems for you in your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: I’d like to say no, but… yeah, it’s started to cause some trouble, mostly at work. Not huge trouble, I don’t think, but enough that people are noticing.\n\nSometimes during room turnovers or when we’re deciding supplies, I’ll miss part of what someone said, or I’ll jump in too quick, and then it rubs people the wrong way. My supervisor’s had to correct me a few times, and he suggested I talk to the employee assistance program. So… I guess that means it’s affecting things more than I wanted to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over these repetitive actions when you're doing them?\n\nPatient: I mean… I can stop if I really notice it, usually. Like if my wife gives me a look when we’re fishing, I’ll put the tackle box down, or at bingo I’ll stop tapping the pencil for a bit.\n\nBut then, if I’m trying to just sit quiet and relax, I catch myself doing it again—tapping, talking louder than I meant to, fiddling with things. So I guess I have some control, but it doesn’t feel like I’m choosing it on purpose. It kind of sneaks back in. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you experience these restless feelings and repetitive actions in most areas of your life, or are they mainly limited to certain situations like work or church?\n\nPatient: It’s not just work, I guess. Work is where it shows the most because I’m supposed to be more… organized, you know?\n\nOn my shifts I’ll go from mopping, to checking trash, to straightening chairs without really stopping, and I tell myself I’m just being productive. But even at home, after work, instead of sitting down, I’ll start puttering in the garage or re-sorting tools.\n\nSo… yeah, it’s kind of in a few places. I don’t mean I’m causing chaos everywhere, but I don’t seem to settle very well. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make any repetitive sounds, like clearing your throat or grunt, when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I clear my throat like anybody would, but I haven’t noticed myself making noises like grunting or anything repetitive like that.\n\nIt’s more something I’d probably have to ask my wife if she’s noticed, but as far as I know, no. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and need to move are usually triggered by feeling worried or apprehensive about something?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s usually worry, no… I mean, sometimes I might be a little keyed up like anybody, but most of the time it’s more like my body just won’t settle when I’m supposed to sit still.\n\nLike in staff meetings, or those church event briefings, I’ll be trying to pay attention and be respectful, but it feels like there’s this motor humming under my skin. I’ll shift around, or stand up if I can, or find some excuse to go move something or check on something.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s not always because I’m scared or apprehensive. It’s more just… sitting still feels hard. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these restless feelings and need to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was some terrible kid or anything, but I do remember getting in trouble in grade school for popping out of my seat.\n\nTeachers would tell me to stop talking over them, or I’d start doing something before they’d even finished giving the directions. My mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” like I just had too much motor in me even then.\n\nSo, yes… I guess it was there when I was a kid too. Is that the kind of childhood stuff you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive actions in response to some kind of thought or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: Not really like a thought, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s not like I’m doing it because I have some idea I can’t get out of my head.\n\nIt’s more just that feeling like I’ve got a motor going under my skin when I’m supposed to sit still. In meetings or those church briefings, I’ll start shifting around, or I’ll stand if I can, or I’ll find some little reason to move. I guess it feels like an urge in my body more than a thought, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're doing these repetitive actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean… I don’t feel like if I don’t shift around or tap my foot then something bad is going to happen.\n\nIt’s more just uncomfortable to sit still, if that makes sense. I’m not trying to stop some disaster or anything like that. I just get restless and end up moving before I really think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and repetitive actions are attempts to reduce anxiety or anxiety-like feelings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s to reduce anxiety, exactly. I mean, I can get uncomfortable if I’m stuck sitting still too long, but it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to calm myself down from worry, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… my body just wants to move. I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it. I guess there can be some tension in it, but I haven’t really noticed it being tied to anxious thoughts or anything like that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit these restless behaviors before you were 7 years old?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make any repetitive sounds, like clearing your throat or grunt, when you're feeling restless?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not really. I mean, I clear my throat like anybody would, but I haven’t noticed myself making noises like grunting or anything repetitive like that.\n\nIt’s more something I’d probably have to ask my wife if she’s noticed, but as far as I know, no. Is that okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your restlessness and need to move are usually triggered by feeling worried or apprehensive about something?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s usually worry, no… I mean, sometimes I might be a little keyed up like anybody, but most of the time it’s more like my body just won’t settle when I’m supposed to sit still.\n\nLike in staff meetings, or those church event briefings, I’ll be trying to pay attention and be respectful, but it feels like there’s this motor humming under my skin. I’ll shift around, or stand up if I can, or find some excuse to go move something or check on something.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but it’s not always because I’m scared or apprehensive. It’s more just… sitting still feels hard. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these restless feelings and need to move when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to make it sound like I was some terrible kid or anything, but I do remember getting in trouble in grade school for popping out of my seat.\n\nTeachers would tell me to stop talking over them, or I’d start doing something before they’d even finished giving the directions. My mother used to say I was “wound too tight,” like I just had too much motor in me even then.\n\nSo, yes… I guess it was there when I was a kid too. Is that the kind of childhood stuff you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these repetitive actions in response to some kind of thought or urge that you can't ignore?\n\nPatient: Not really like a thought, no… at least I don’t think so. It’s not like I’m doing it because I have some idea I can’t get out of my head.\n\nIt’s more just that feeling like I’ve got a motor going under my skin when I’m supposed to sit still. In meetings or those church briefings, I’ll start shifting around, or I’ll stand if I can, or I’ll find some little reason to move. I guess it feels like an urge in my body more than a thought, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're doing these repetitive actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean… I don’t feel like if I don’t shift around or tap my foot then something bad is going to happen.\n\nIt’s more just uncomfortable to sit still, if that makes sense. I’m not trying to stop some disaster or anything like that. I just get restless and end up moving before I really think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and repetitive actions are attempts to reduce anxiety or anxiety-like feelings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s to reduce anxiety, exactly. I mean, I can get uncomfortable if I’m stuck sitting still too long, but it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to calm myself down from worry, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… my body just wants to move. I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it. I guess there can be some tension in it, but I haven’t really noticed it being tied to anxious thoughts or anything like that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit these restless behaviors before you were 7 years old?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t remember everything from being that little, but in grade school I did get in trouble for popping up out of my seat and talking over the teacher.\n\nI’d also kind of rush ahead before they were done giving directions. Not trying to be bad, I don’t think… just impatient, I guess. My mother used to say I was “wound too tight” even as a kid. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these actions to make yourself feel just right or to follow a specific rule?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. It’s not like I have a rule in my head that I have to tap a certain number of times or move a certain way.\n\nIt’s more just… I get uncomfortable sitting still, and then I shift or get up before I really think about it. I’m not trying to make it feel “just right,” at least not that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a strong need to perform these repetitive actions in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might tap my foot or shift around, but I don’t feel like it has to be done a certain way or exactly right.\n\nIt’s more just something I catch myself doing. If someone told me to stop, I might be uncomfortable or distracted, but I don’t think I’d feel like I broke some rule or had to start over. Is that what you’re getting at?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers notice you exhibiting these restless behaviors or repetitive actions before you started elementary school?\n\nPatient: Before elementary school, I’m not totally sure what teachers would’ve seen, if any. But my mother did say even when I was pretty young I acted like I was “wound too tight,” always kind of on the go.\n\nAnd once I was in grade school, yeah, teachers definitely noticed—getting on me for popping out of my seat, talking over them, rushing ahead before they finished directions. I don’t mean I was trying to be a problem… that’s just how it came out. Is that the kind of early thing you’re asking about?\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these restless feelings and need to move for more than 6 months?\n\nPatient: Yes… I mean, definitely more than six months. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but this rougher stretch has been going on about a year and five months now.\n\nIt really seemed to pick up after my cleaning contract changed—rotating hours and more event work. Since then, the restless feeling and needing to move around has been more noticeable. Is that the kind of timeframe you’re asking for?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I guess it feels like an urge in my body more than a thought, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're doing these repetitive actions to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean… I don’t feel like if I don’t shift around or tap my foot then something bad is going to happen.\n\nIt’s more just uncomfortable to sit still, if that makes sense. I’m not trying to stop some disaster or anything like that. I just get restless and end up moving before I really think about it.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your restlessness and repetitive actions are attempts to reduce anxiety or anxiety-like feelings?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s to reduce anxiety, exactly. I mean, I can get uncomfortable if I’m stuck sitting still too long, but it doesn’t feel like I’m trying to calm myself down from worry, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s more like… my body just wants to move. I don’t know if that’s the right way to put it. I guess there can be some tension in it, but I haven’t really noticed it being tied to anxious thoughts or anything like that. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit these restless behaviors before you were 7 years old?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t remember everything from being that little, but in grade school I did get in trouble for popping up out of my seat and talking over the teacher.\n\nI’d also kind of rush ahead before they were done giving directions. Not trying to be bad, I don’t think… just impatient, I guess. My mother used to say I was “wound too tight” even as a kid. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these actions to make yourself feel just right or to follow a specific rule?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. It’s not like I have a rule in my head that I have to tap a certain number of times or move a certain way.\n\nIt’s more just… I get uncomfortable sitting still, and then I shift or get up before I really think about it. I’m not trying to make it feel “just right,” at least not that I’ve noticed. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt a strong need to perform these repetitive actions in a very specific way?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I might tap my foot or shift around, but I don’t feel like it has to be done a certain way or exactly right.\n\nIt’s more just something I catch myself doing. If someone told me to stop, I might be uncomfortable or distracted, but I don’t think I’d feel like I broke some rule or had to start over. Is that what you’re getting at?\n\nDoctor: Did your parents or teachers notice you exhibiting these restless behaviors or repetitive actions before you started elementary school?\n\nPatient: Before elementary school, I’m not totally sure what teachers would’ve seen, if any. I don’t mean I was trying to be a problem… that’s just how it came out.
